Title: Wasserstein gradient flows of Maximum Mean Discrepancy with energy kernels

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.01182v1

Published Time: Tue, 04 Aug 2026 01:18:34 GMT

Number of Pages: 78

Markdown Content:
WASSERSTEIN GRADIENT FLOWS OF MAXIMUM MEAN DISCREPANCY WITH ENERGY KERNELS 

MATTHEW ROSENZWEIG, DEJAN SLEPČEV AND LIHAN WANG Abstract. We study the Wasserstein gradient flow of the squared Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD) generated by the nonsmooth energy kernels 𝐾(𝑧) = −|𝑧| 𝑞 , 0 < 𝑞 < 2 . In dimensions 𝑑 ≥ 2 ,the corresponding energies are not displacement semiconvex, so standard Wasserstein-gradient-flow theory does not apply. When 𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2 > 0 , we prove global well-posedness on ℝ𝑑 for probability densities in subcritical 𝐿 𝑝 spaces, with targets in the same integrability class and with finite moments. We also include the one-dimensional Coulomb endpoint 𝑑 = 𝑞 = 1 .For the associated 𝑁 -particle system, we prove global noncollision and fixed-𝑁 convergence to the collision-free critical set, a particle-to-continuum criticality principle, and a modulated-energy mean-field estimate that yields convergence of the particle dynamics to the continuum flow as 𝑁 → ∞ on every finite time interval. We also construct collision-free saddle equilibria, showing that deterministic particle trajectories need not approach global empirical minimizers. For 1 ≤ 𝑞 < 2 , every continuum solution in our class has a narrowly relatively compact orbit, every 𝜔 -limit point is Lagrangian critical, and the orbit approaches the Lagrangian critical set. For 

0 < 𝑞 < 1 , the same conclusions hold under uniform-in-time moment and subcritical 𝐿 𝑝 bounds. We prove that an absolutely continuous Lagrangian critical point equals the target when the source and target have finite moments of order 𝑞 , except when 0 < 𝑞 < 1 and 𝑑 ∈ {1, 3} . In the residual three-dimensional regime, rigidity holds under a finite (𝑞 + 1) -th moment, compactness of the positive part of the discrepancy, or radiality of the discrepancy, whereas in dimension one explicit nested-interval examples yield non-minimizing critical points. Under the preceding uniform bounds, rigidity gives convergence of the continuum flow to the target throughout the rigid part of the well-posedness range. Finally, we show that no initial-data-independent multiplicative MMD decay modulus exists on ℝ𝑑 , and that global Polyak–Łojasiewicz inequalities fail in several whole-space and periodic Riesz/Coulomb regimes. 

1. Introduction Maximum mean discrepancy rose to prominence as a practical nonparametric criterion for two-sample testing [Gre+07, Gre+12]. Its Wasserstein gradient flow formally transports a source distri-bution toward a prescribed target and provides a dynamical model for statistical machine learning, sampling, and generative learning [Arb+19, AHS23, Her+24b, Hag+24]. In data-driven applica-tions, the target distribution 𝜇 is typically replaced by its empirical distribution 𝜇 𝑁 .Let 𝜇 be a target probability measure on ℝ𝑑 .1 We study the energy MMD generated by (1.1) 𝐾(𝑧) = −|𝑧| 𝑞 , 0 < 𝑞 < 2. 

We refer to this negative-distance family as the Euclidean energy kernels; for 𝑞 = 1 , the associ-ated MMD is, up to normalization, the classical energy distance [SR05, Szé03]. Its centered-kernel definition, moment requirements, and the significance of the threshold 𝑞 = 2 are recalled at the beginning of Section 1.1. The formal continuum flow is (1.2) 𝜕 𝑡 𝜌 𝑡 = −∇ ⋅ (𝜌 𝑡 𝑣 𝑡 ), 𝑣 𝑡 = −∇𝐾 ∗ (𝜌 𝑡 − 𝜇), 

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification. 35Q70, 49Q22, 35B40 (Primary); 35R09, 46E22, 60B10, 82C22 (Secondary). 

Key words and phrases. Maximum mean discrepancy, Wasserstein gradient flow, energy kernels, Riesz potentials, aggregation equation, mean-field limit, modulated energy, interacting particle system, Lagrangian critical point, long-time behavior. and, writing [𝑁] ∶= {1, … , 𝑁} , its (diagonal-free) particle counterpart is (1.3) ̇ 𝑥 𝑡 𝑖 = − 1𝑁 ∑

> 𝑗∈[𝑁]⧵{𝑖}

∇𝐾(𝑥 𝑡 𝑖 − 𝑥 𝑡 𝑗 ) + ∇(𝐾 ∗ 𝜇)(𝑥 𝑡 𝑖 ), 𝑖 ∈ [𝑁]. 

The missing self-interaction in (1.3) is part of the model, necessary to allow for singularities in the velocity field. We use ∇𝐾(0) = 0 in this paper only as shorthand for this convention. Since the continuum theory concerns 𝐿 𝑝 densities while empirical measures are atomic, the two evolutions must be constructed separately. For these nonsmooth kernels, standard Wasserstein-gradient-flow and mean-field theories do not apply directly: the interaction force fails to be Lipschitz at the particle diagonal for every 0 < 𝑞 < 2 , and in dimensions 𝑑 ≥ 2 the energies are not displacement semiconvex. Our work touches on various aspects of both the continnum flow and the interacting particles and connects them quantitatively. Our main contributions are in the following five directions. i. Global well-posedness of the continuum flow. In the range 𝑑+𝑞−2 > 0 we prove existence and uniqueness of a global weak solution of (1.2) for probability densities in subcritical 𝐿 𝑝 

spaces with finite moments (that is, 𝑝 > 𝑝 𝑐 ∶= 𝑑∕(𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2) ; see (1.11)), with a velocity field that is Lipschitz in space uniformly on bounded time intervals, together with the associated energy–dissipation identity. The one-dimensional Coulomb endpoint 𝑑 = 𝑞 = 1 is included and follows from the stronger Cauchy theory of [CR26b]. See Theorem 1.2. ii. Particle dynamics. For the system (1.3) started from any pairwise distinct configuration, we prove global noncollision with uniform-in-time separation and boundedness, and convergence at fixed 𝑁 to the collision-free critical set of the particle energy (Theorem 1.5). We prove a particle-to-continuum criticality principle (Proposition 4.3) and uniform-in-time consistency for well-prepared data (Corollary 4.9). Explicit collision-free saddle equilibria (Proposition 1.6) show that deterministic trajectories need not approach global empirical minimizers. iii. Mean-field limit. We prove a modulated-energy estimate (Theorem 1.8): a quantitative com-parison, in squared MMD, of the empirical measure of the particle flow with the continuum solution driven by the same target. A probability measure is approximated by the empirical measure of 𝑁 independent samples with MMD error of order 𝑁 −1∕2 , at a rate that does not depend on the dimension. The estimate therefore yields convergence of the particle dynamics to the continuum flow as 𝑁 → ∞ on every finite time interval, at the same rate 𝑁 −1∕2 for such well-prepared random initial data. iv. Stationary states and long-time behavior. We obtain a near-complete stationary picture among absolutely continuous states (Theorem 1.4): at the natural moment level, a Lagrangian critical point (one at which the velocity field ∇𝐾 ∗ (𝜌 − 𝜇) vanishes 𝜌 -almost everywhere; see Definition 5.1, and Section 5 for the comparison with the Wasserstein notion of [BV25]) equals the target except when 0 < 𝑞 < 1 and 𝑑 ∈ {1, 3} ; in the residual three-dimensional regime, rigidity holds under a finite moment of order 𝑞 + 1 , compactness of the positive part of the discrepancy, or radiality of the discrepancy about some point; and in dimension one explicit nested-interval examples yield non-minimizing critical points. Combined with the energy– dissipation identity, this gives asymptotic criticality without additional long-time bounds for 

1 ≤ 𝑞 < 2 , and conditional convergence to the target throughout the rigid part of the present well-posedness range (Theorem 5.24 and Corollary 5.25). v. Obstructions to uniform convergence rates. We construct explicit examples showing that uniform convergence rates and global Polyak–Łojasiewicz inequalities cannot hold. Dynam-ically, for every (𝑑, 𝑞) in the well-posedness range and every admissible target, translating a fixed compactly supported source rules out any multiplicative MMD decay modulus indepen-dent of the initial datum (Proposition 1.11). Statically, no global PL inequality holds on ℝ𝑑 

for any 0 < 𝑞 < 2 and any compactly supported target, and the same construction covers the negative-order Riesz range −𝑑 < 𝑞 < 0 (Proposition 6.2). On 𝕋 𝑑 , where spatial escape is un-available, a high-frequency construction gives the same failure for 2 − 𝑑 < 𝑞 < 2 and every bounded, uniformly positive target (Proposition 6.4); this range is sharp, since at the Coulomb endpoint 𝑞 = 2 − 𝑑 a PL inequality can instead hold. These are statements about uniformity WASSERSTEIN GRADIENT FLOWS OF MAXIMUM MEAN DISCREPANCY WITH ENERGY KERNELS 3

over the whole admissible class, not about any individual solution. The mechanism is trans-port at finite speed: a source at distance 𝑅 from the target is driven by a far-field velocity of order 𝑅 𝑞−1 , so it requires a time of order 𝑅 2−𝑞 merely to reach the target before any relaxation can begin, while its squared discrepancy remains of order 𝑅 𝑞 throughout. What is excluded is a single decay modulus valid simultaneously at every initial separation; rates depending on the initial support scale, or holding after a corresponding waiting time, are not excluded. Section 8 exhibits precisely this behavior. Finally, in the one-dimensional 𝑞 = 1 particle model, we exhibit a two-time-scale phenomenon: particles outside the target support first travel toward it and then relax exponentially, provided the target density is bounded below on its support. A second example shows that this positivity cannot be dropped: for a target vanishing at an interior point, a particle started inside the support relaxes only polynomially. We also sharpen the description of the topology metrized by MMD 𝑞 : it dominates every transport cost of order 𝑟 < 𝑞∕2 (Proposition A.4), and this threshold is sharp, confirming a conjecture of Modeste and Dombry [MD24] that convergence in MMD 𝑞 need not imply convergence in the 𝑞∕2 -transport topology (Example A.5). 

Remark 1.1 (The role of the exponent 𝑞 ). The exponent enters in two independent ways, and the two thresholds do not coincide. Metrically, Lemma 2.1 identifies MMD 𝑞 with the ̇ 𝐻 −(𝑑+𝑞)∕2 norm, so 𝑞 tunes the strength of the discrepancy: smaller 𝑞 gives a stronger norm and finer resolution, while 𝑞 = 2 is degenerate, the centered kernel reducing to 𝑘 2(𝑥, 𝑦) = 𝑥 ⋅ 𝑦 and the discrepancy detecting only the difference of means. Analytically, our hypotheses change at 𝑞 = 1 . For 1 ≤ 𝑞 < 2 , asymptotic criticality holds with no additional long-time bounds (Theorem 5.24), rigidity at the natural moment level holds in every dimension (Theorem 1.4(i)), the particle-to-continuum criticality principle requires no microscopic nonconcentration hypothesis (Proposition 4.3), and the interaction force is bounded at the diagonal. For 0 < 𝑞 < 1 each of these acquires an additional hypothesis. The rigidity exceptions are confined to 𝑑 ∈ {1, 3} , so in even dimensions and in odd dimensions 𝑑 ≥ 5 rigidity holds throughout 0 < 𝑞 < 2 . In applications 𝑞 is generally chosen on modeling grounds; the above is not a recommendation, but a record of which regimes the present guarantees cover. 1.1. Background. The centered expression in (1.6) is finite for pairs of measures in 𝒫 𝑞∕2 (ℝ 𝑑 ). Under the stronger hypothesis 𝜌, 𝜇 ∈ 𝒫 𝑞 (ℝ 𝑑 ), the translation-invariant representation (1.7) is available [Sej+13]. The 4 MATTHEW ROSENZWEIG, DEJAN SLEPČEV AND LIHAN WANG 

well-posedness theory below assumes moments of order 𝑟 ≥ 1 ; since 𝑞∕2 < 1 , this ensures finiteness of the centered MMD. We use the translation-invariant representation only when the 𝒫 𝑞 hypothesis is explicitly available. Taking 𝑘 = 𝑘 𝑞 in (1.4) recovers the centered definition (1.6). The test class is then concrete: since 

(𝑑 + 𝑞)∕2 > 𝑑∕2 for every 𝑞 > 0 , the RKHS of 𝑘 𝑞 is the homogeneous Sobolev space ̇ 𝐻 (𝑑+𝑞)∕2 (ℝ 𝑑 )

modulo constants, and the constants are immaterial because 𝜌 − 𝜇 has zero mass; see Remark 2.2. The threshold 𝑞 = 2 marks the degenerate endpoint of this family: −|𝑥 − 𝑦| 2 remains conditionally positive semidefinite, but 𝑘 2(𝑥, 𝑦) = 𝑥 ⋅ 𝑦 , so the resulting discrepancy detects only the difference between the means of 𝜌 and 𝜇 . Beyond 𝑞 = 2 , conditional positive semidefiniteness fails in general. Thus 0 < 𝑞 < 2 is the nondegenerate negative-distance MMD range; see [Sej+13]. The Fourier– Sobolev representation in Lemma 2.1 identifies MMD 𝑞 with a negative homogeneous Sobolev norm. Several features make these kernels attractive in statistics and computation. On 𝒫 𝑞∕2 (ℝ 𝑑 ), con-vergence in MMD 𝑞 implies weak convergence; indeed, it implies convergence in every transport cost of order 0 < 𝑟 < 𝑞∕2 , as recorded in Proposition A.4; see also [Sim+23, Sej+13]. The restric-tion 0 < 𝑟 < 𝑞∕2 is sharp: Example A.5 confirms the conjecture of Modeste and Dombry [MD24, Theorem 13 and the paragraph following it] that MMD convergence need not imply convergence in the 𝑞∕2 -transport topology. For the unbounded energy kernel considered here and 𝜇 ∈ 𝒫 𝑞 (ℝ 𝑑 ),empirical MMD has the dimension-free 𝑁 −1∕2 Monte Carlo scaling familiar from standard MMD theory [Gre+07, Gre+12, TSM17]. More precisely, Proposition A.1 gives an exact formula of order 

𝑁 −1 for the expected squared MMD of the empirical measure together with a high-probability con-sequence. This contrasts with the dimension-dependent empirical rates for Wasserstein distances [FG15] and motivates MMD gradient flows and their particle approximations. Unlike smooth bounded kernels, the energy kernel remains sensitive at large spatial scales: since 

|𝑧| 𝑞 grows at infinity, the discrepancy does not saturate when source and target mass are widely separated. This feature contributes to the practical interest in these kernels in generative learning and sampling; see [AHS23, Her+24b, Hag+24]. A further computational advantage, observed by Kolouri, Nadjahi, Shahrampour, and Şimşekli [Kol+22] and exploited by Hertrich, Wald, Altekrüger, and Hagemann [Her+24b], is the sliced iden-tity. For 𝜉 ∈ 𝕊 𝑑−1 , let 𝑃 𝜉 (𝑥) ∶= 𝜉 ⋅ 𝑥 , and let 𝒰 𝕊 𝑑−1 denote the uniform probability measure on 𝕊 𝑑−1 .Then (1.9) MMD 2𝑞 (𝜌, 𝜇) = 𝐶 𝑑,𝑞 𝔼 𝜉∼𝒰 𝕊 𝑑−1 

[MMD 2𝑞 ((𝑃 𝜉 )#𝜌, (𝑃 𝜉 )#𝜇) ].

In practice, one samples directions 𝜉 , projects the source and target samples, and evaluates the resulting one-dimensional discrepancies and gradients. In the energy-distance case 𝑞 = 1 , sorting the one-dimensional projections gives 𝑂((𝑀 + 𝑁) log(𝑀 + 𝑁)) complexity per sampled direction, instead of the naive 𝑂(𝑁 2 + 𝑀𝑁) per-step cost after precomputing the target–target term. At short scales, however, the force creates an analytical difficulty: although the particle-particle force −∇𝐾(𝑧) = 𝑞|𝑧| 𝑞−2 𝑧 is repulsive, it fails to be Lipschitz at the particle diagonal throughout 

0 < 𝑞 < 2 ; the force itself is singular only when 0 < 𝑞 < 1 . This distinction is relevant to the diagonal-free particle dynamics and the noncollision result in Theorem 1.5. At the level of the continuum flow, the kernels present a second obstruction. For interaction profiles 𝐾 such that (𝑥, 𝑦) ↦ 𝐾(𝑥 − 𝑦) is jointly semiconvex in the 𝑥 and 𝑦 variables, the standard theory of Wasserstein gradient flows in the space of probability measures [AGS08] provides well-posedness of (1.2), allows for general measure-valued solutions including discrete measures, and gives stability estimates that imply mean-field convergence of (1.3) to (1.2). These tools do not apply directly to the energy kernels above. In one dimension, the fixed-target MMD functional associated with 𝐾(𝑥 − 𝑦) = −|𝑥 − 𝑦| is displacement convex, and this flow was analyzed through quantiles by Duong, Stein, Beinert, Hertrich, and Steidl [Duo+26]; see also the work of Bonaschi, Carrillo, Di Francesco, and Peletier [Bon+15] who treated the pure source–source interaction energy. This is an endpoint phenomenon: for every 0 < 𝑞 < 1 , the corresponding fixed-target functional is not displacement convex, although its pure source–source interaction term is. In dimensions 𝑑 ≥ 2 , the energies are likewise not displacement semiconvex, so well-posedness in the full space of probability WASSERSTEIN GRADIENT FLOWS OF MAXIMUM MEAN DISCREPANCY WITH ENERGY KERNELS 5

measures is not available from the standard theory. The remaining range considered here instead requires the 𝐿 𝑝 approach described next. 1.2. Main results. We state the results in the order of the objects they concern rather than in the order of the contributions listed above: first the continuum flow, through its well-posedness, its stationary picture, and its long-time behavior; then the particle system and the mean-field estimate linking the two; and finally the obstructions to uniform convergence. We begin with global well-posedness for the continuum flow (1.2). The one-dimensional Coulomb endpoint 𝑑 = 𝑞 = 1 is included for completeness and follows from the stronger Cauchy theory of Chodron de Courcel and the first author [CR26b]. In the remaining range 𝑑+𝑞 −2 > 0 , we work in Lebesgue classes and adapt the aggregation-equation method of Bertozzi–Laurent–Rosado [BLR11] to the additional target field ∇𝐾 ∗ 𝜇 .For 𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2 ≥ 0 , we set, here and throughout, (1.10) 𝑝 𝑐 ∶= ⎧⎨⎩𝑑 𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2 , 𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2 > 0, ∞, 𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2 = 0. 

Theorem 1.2 (Global well-posedness) . Let 𝑟 ≥ 1 , let 0 < 𝑞 < 2 satisfy 𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2 ≥ 0 , and let 

(1.11) 𝑝 𝑐 ≤ 𝑝 ≤ ∞, and 𝑝 > 𝑝 𝑐 if 𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2 > 0. 

If 𝜇, 𝜌 0 ∈ 𝒫 𝑟 (ℝ 𝑑 ) ∩ 𝐿 𝑝 (ℝ 𝑑 ) are probability densities, then (1.2) has a unique global weak solution in the class of Definition 3.1. For every 𝑇 > 0 , the 𝑟 -th moment remains bounded on [0, 𝑇] and 

(1.12) 𝑣 𝑡 = −∇𝐾 ∗ (𝜌 𝑡 − 𝜇), 𝑣 ∈ 𝐿 ∞([0, 𝑇]; 𝑊 1,∞ (ℝ 𝑑 )) ∩ 𝐶([0, 𝑇] × ℝ 𝑑 ).

Remark 1.3 (Well-posedness hypotheses) . Let 𝑝 ∗ denote the Hölder conjugate of 𝑝 . When 𝑑+𝑞−2 > 0, condition (1.11) is equivalent to (2 − 𝑞)𝑝 ∗ < 𝑑 ; it is exactly the subcritical integrability needed to convolve the Hessian of the kernel |𝑥| 𝑞−2 with 𝐿 𝑝 densities. For the target-free equation, 𝑝 𝑐 is the 

𝐿 𝑝 -critical exponent for the time-preserving scaling (1.14) 𝜌(𝑡, 𝑥) ⟼ 𝜆 𝑑+𝑞−2 𝜌(𝑡, 𝜆𝑥), 

which leaves the 𝐿 𝑝 𝑐 norm invariant while rescaling the total mass. The finite critical endpoint 

𝑝 = 𝑝 𝑐 < ∞ remains excluded; we return to the open problem of including it in Section 9. In this strict dimensional range, the case 𝑝 = ∞ is obtained from any admissible finite exponent and the maximum principle. If 𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2 = 0 , then necessarily 𝑑 = 𝑞 = 1 , and (1.11) forces 𝑝 = 𝑝 𝑐 = ∞ ;this Coulomb endpoint follows instead from [CR26b, Theorem 1.2 and Proposition 2.1]. We turn to the stationary picture: a nearly complete rigidity–flexibility classification for abso-lutely continuous Lagrangian critical points. 

Theorem 1.4 (Lagrangian critical-point rigidity and flexibility) . Let 𝑑 ≥ 1 and 0 < 𝑞 < 2 . The following statements hold. 

(i) Let 𝜇, 𝜌 ∈ 𝒫 𝑞 (ℝ 𝑑 ), assume 𝜌 ≪ ℒ 𝑑 , and suppose that 𝜌 is a Lagrangian critical point of the MMD energy with target 𝜇 in the sense of Definition 5.1. Then 𝜌 = 𝜇 in any of the following cases: 

(a) 𝑑 is even; 

(b) 𝑑 is odd and 1 ≤ 𝑞 < 2 ;

(c) 𝑑 ≥ 5 is odd and 0 < 𝑞 < 1 .

(ii) If 𝑑 = 3 and 0 < 𝑞 < 1 , under the measure and criticality hypotheses of (i) , the conclusion 

𝜌 = 𝜇 also holds provided that at least one of the following conditions is satisfied: 

(a) 𝜌, 𝜇 ∈ 𝒫 𝑞+1 (ℝ 3);

(b) the positive part of the signed discrepancy, (𝜌 − 𝜇) +, has compact support; 6 MATTHEW ROSENZWEIG, DEJAN SLEPČEV AND LIHAN WANG 

(c) 𝜌 − 𝜇 is invariant under rotations about some point. The compact-positive-part condition holds, in particular, if supp 𝜌 is compact. 

(iii) If 𝑑 = 1 and 0 < 𝑞 < 1 , then for every 0 < 𝑟 < 𝑅 there exist distinct compactly supported absolutely continuous probability measures 𝜌 𝑟 , 𝜇 ∈ 𝒫 𝑞 (ℝ) satisfying 

(1.15) supp 𝜌 𝑟 = [−𝑟, 𝑟] ⊊ [−𝑅, 𝑅] = supp 𝜇 

such that 𝜌 𝑟 is a Lagrangian critical point for the target 𝜇 , but is not a minimizer. 

Part (i) follows from Theorem 5.4, the three alternatives in part (ii) follow from Theorem 5.17, and part (iii) is proved in Proposition 5.22. Thus any unresolved natural-𝑞 -moment case in dimension three must have nonradial discrepancy and unbounded support of its positive part. The energy–dissipation identity for the solution in Theorem 1.2 also yields long-time asymptotic criticality. For 1 ≤ 𝑞 < 2 , every sequence of times tending to infinity has a subsequence along which the flow converges narrowly to a Lagrangian critical point, and the full orbit approaches the Lagrangian critical set, without any additional long-time bound. For 0 < 𝑞 < 1 , the same conclusions hold under uniform moment and 𝐿 𝑝 bounds. Under those bounds, if the available moment order meets the corresponding rigidity hypothesis, every 𝜔 -limit point equals the target and the full trajectory converges to it. Within the range of Theorem 1.2, this applies throughout part (i) of Theorem 1.4 and also for 𝑑 = 3 and 0 < 𝑞 < 1 either under the additional-moment alternative in part (ii) or when the initial source and target are radial about a common center; see Theorem 5.24 and Corollary 5.25. These conclusions are qualitative and no rate is asserted. Before stating the mean-field estimate, we establish global noncollision and fixed-𝑁 asymptotic criticality for the particle dynamics, including the one-dimensional range 0 < 𝑞 < 1 , where unique-ness is not asserted. We also prove uniform-in-time consistency for well-prepared particle data: if

Remark 1.7 (Uniqueness for regular targets) . In case (iii) of Theorem 1.5, uniqueness is not asserted because the assumption 𝑝 > 1∕𝑞 guarantees continuity, but not an Osgood modulus of continuity, for 𝐾 ′ ∗ 𝜇 . If, in addition, 𝜇 ∈ 𝐶 𝛼 𝑏 (ℝ) for some 1−𝑞 ≤ 𝛼 < 1 , then uniqueness does hold. Indeed, the standard Hölder–Zygmund estimates for fractional potentials [Ste70, Chapter V] show that 𝐾 ′ ∗ 𝜇 

is locally Lipschitz when 𝛼 > 1 − 𝑞 , while for 𝛼 = 1 − 𝑞 it belongs to the Zygmund class and is therefore locally log-Lipschitz. Since the pair-interaction field is smooth on 𝒟 𝑁 , the full particle vector field has an Osgood modulus on compact subsets of 𝒟 𝑁 . The Osgood uniqueness criterion [Tes12, Problem 2.25] gives uniqueness up to the first collision, and the noncollision argument in the proof below then yields a unique global solution. Because the standard Dobrushin–Sznitman coupling argument relies on a uniform Lipschitz bound for the interaction force, it does not apply directly here, where |∇ ⊗2 𝐾(𝑥)| ∼ |𝑥| 𝑞−2 at the particle diagonal [Dob79, Szn91]. Adapted Wasserstein methods can nevertheless treat certain singular interactions: for divergence-free Euler-type kernels, Hauray [Hau09] obtains a quantita-tive 𝑊 ∞ estimate under a coupled initial 𝑊 ∞-approximation and minimum-separation condition. Those structural and configuration hypotheses do not directly furnish the deterministic MMD es-timate proved here for arbitrary pairwise-distinct configurations. The relative-entropy framework of Jabin–Wang [JW18] is likewise not directly applicable: it compares an 𝑁 -particle law with a ten-sorized continuum law rather than a single deterministic empirical trajectory, and its main estimates are formulated in the periodic setting. The whole-space extensions discussed there do not cover the growth |∇𝐾(𝑥)| ∼ |𝑥| 𝑞−1 of the interaction field at infinity when 𝑞 > 1 .We instead follow the modulated-energy method introduced by Duerinckx and Serfaty [Due16, Ser20] and further developed by Q. H. Nguyen, Serfaty, and the first author [NRS22]. This latter work is closest to the present setting, and the method entails differentiating the modulated MMD energy and controlling the resulting transport commutator through the homogeneous Sobolev rep-resentation of MMD. For overviews of the method and its commutator formulation, see [Ser26, Ros25]. Ultimately, we obtain the following mean-field estimate.

Remark 1.9 (One-dimensional super-Coulomb range) . The restriction above excludes the range 𝑑 = 1 and 0 < 𝑞 < 1 only in the presence of a nonzero target. When 𝜇 = 0 , the mean-field conclusion continues to hold by combining the preceding commutator estimate with the local classical well-posedness theory of Choi–Jeong [CJ21]; foundational weak and regularity theories for the target-free fractional porous medium equation were developed in [CV11, CSV13, BIK15]. A central long-time question is whether 𝜌 𝑡 → 𝜇 and at what rate. For 1 ≤ 𝑞 < 2 , Theo-rem 5.24 gives qualitative convergence to the Lagrangian critical point set without additional long-time bounds, but it does not identify a possibly singular limit point with the target. The gap is a single estimate: under the uniform-in-time bound (5.95), every 𝜔 -limit point is an 𝐿 𝑝 density with finite 𝑟 -th moment, and rigidity then identifies it with 𝜇 (Corollary 5.25). Whether that bound prop-agates along the flow is the first question raised in Section 9. When 0 < 𝑞 < 1 , asymptotic criticality holds under uniform moment and 𝐿 𝑝 bounds. We also show that, for every target covered by the well-posedness theorem, no multiplicative MMD decay modulus can be uniform over the full ad-missible class of initial data. 

Proposition 1.11 (No universal global MMD convergence rate) . Let 𝑟 ≥ 1 , let 0 < 𝑞 < 2 satisfy

(1.27) MMD 2𝑞 (𝜌 𝑡 , 𝜇) ≤ 𝛾(𝑡)MMD 2𝑞 (𝜌 0, 𝜇), 𝑡 ≥ 0. 

The obstruction already appears along translations of a fixed compactly supported probability density: the solution remains in a far-field neighborhood of its initial location for a time 𝑇 𝑅 → ∞ ,while its squared MMD from the fixed target remains comparable to 𝑅 𝑞 .It therefore rules out rates uniform over the initial support scale, but does not rule out qualitative convergence or estimates after a datum-dependent waiting time. The remaining results of this paper describe other mechanisms that can and cannot drive conver-gence. Section 5 develops the rigidity mechanisms and the nested-interval construction underlying Theorem 1.4, and then proves asymptotic criticality, unconditionally for 1 ≤ 𝑞 < 2 and under uni-form bounds for 0 < 𝑞 < 1 , together with conditional convergence to the target in the rigid part of the present well-posedness range. Section 6 proves further whole-space and periodic failures of global PL inequalities. Further metric and completion results for MMD 𝑞 are collected in Appen-dix A. 1.3. Prior and companion work. For smooth kernels, Arbel, Korba, Salim, and Gretton [Arb+19] proved well-posedness and mean-field approximation, and discussed conditions under which long-time convergence can hold, while Chen, Mustafi, Glaser, Korba, Gretton, and Sriperumbudur [Che+25] studied a regularized variant. Galashov, De Bortoli, and Gretton [GDG25] developed learned-kernel MMD flows. Related Wasserstein mean-field dynamics arise in neural-network training [MMM19, RV22, SS20, CB18, TS24]. For energy kernels, Kolouri, Nadjahi, Shahrampour, and Şimşekli [Kol+22] and Hertrich, Wald, Altekrüger, and Hagemann [Her+24b] developed the sliced approach reviewed above. Hertrich, Gräf, Beinert, and Steidl [Her+24a] studied Jordan–Kinderlehrer–Otto (JKO) [JKO98] steps for Riesz discrepancies, and Duong, Stein, Beinert, Hertrich, and Steidl [Duo+26] analyzed the one-dimensional distance-kernel flow through quantiles. The theoretical difficulties caused by the non-smoothness of the kernel at the diagonal led Rux, Quellmalz, and Steidl [RQS26] to mollify the negative distance kernel to make it Lipschitz differentiable, and therefore accessible to the standard Wasserstein-gradient-flow theory, while retaining conditional positive semidefiniteness, nearly lin-ear growth at infinity, and the slicing structure. The present work is complementary: rather than regularizing the kernel, we establish the corresponding properties of the flow for the nonsmooth energy kernels themselves. Boufadène and Vialard [BV25] study the Wasserstein gradient flow of the Coulomb discrepancy and emphasize that the Coulomb energy is not geodesically convex in Wasserstein geometry, so global convergence cannot be read off from the standard convex theory. Their notions of Wasserstein and Lagrangian criticality, in particular their Definitions 3.1–3.2 and Theorem 3.4, are used below as a reference point for our stationary-state discussion in Section 5. Building on their odd-dimensional energy-distance rigidity result, we establish rigidity for absolutely continuous Lagrangian critical points in every natural-moment regime except 𝑑 ∈ {1, 3} and 0 < 𝑞 < 1 . The three-dimensional exception is covered under an additional moment hypothesis, when the positive part is compactly supported, or when the discrepancy is radial, while the one-dimensional nested-interval construc-tion shows that uniqueness fails. Our PL counterexamples are complementary: uniqueness of abso-lutely continuous critical points does not by itself furnish a global functional inequality or a uniform convergence rate. A closely related work by Chizat, Colombo, Colombo, and Fernández-Real [Chi+26] studied Wasserstein gradient flows of kernel mean discrepancies on the torus for Sobolev/Riesz kernels, (1.29) ℰ𝜈 𝑠 (𝜌) = 12 ‖𝜌 − 𝜈‖ 2̇ 𝐻 −𝑠 (𝕋 𝑑 ).

Under the identification 𝑠 = (𝑑 + 𝑞)∕2 , the negative-distance kernel considered here corresponds to the range 𝑠 ∈ (𝑑∕2, 𝑑∕2+1) . Their well-posedness theory is formulated in a critical Lorentz class on 

𝕋 𝑑 , whereas Theorem 1.2 gives global Euclidean solutions in finite subcritical 𝐿 𝑝 classes and at the endpoint 𝐿 ∞, with moment assumptions used to control the target field at infinity. Their main quan-titative results concern convergence to the target on compact geometries: exponential convergence in the Coulomb case 𝑠 = 1 and local polynomial convergence for 𝑠 > 1 under positivity, regularity, and small-discrepancy assumptions. The two sets of results meet exactly at 𝑠 = 1 . Indeed, 𝑠 = 1 is the condition 𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2 = 0 , so their Coulomb case meets the present family only at the endpoint 

𝑑 = 𝑞 = 1 , whereas 𝑠 > 1 is exactly the strict well-posedness range 𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2 > 0 of Theorem 1.2, and exactly the range 2 − 𝑑 < 𝑞 < 2 of Proposition 6.4. This explains why their exponential rate does not extend past the endpoint: for every 𝑠 > 1 , which is precisely the regime in which they obtain only local polynomial convergence, Proposition 6.4 rules out a global PL inequality on 𝕋 𝑑 ,so the standard route to exponential decay is unavailable there. On ℝ𝑑 , Propositions 1.11 and 6.2 10 MATTHEW ROSENZWEIG, DEJAN SLEPČEV AND LIHAN WANG 

likewise exclude any initial-data-independent MMD decay modulus and any global PL inequality. In addition to these continuum questions, the present paper treats diagonal-free particle dynamics; the noncollision and modulated-energy propagation estimates in Theorems 1.5 and 1.8 are separate from the torus convergence analysis in [Chi+26]. Another closely related work, by Chodron de Courcel and the first author [CR26b], studies Wasserstein gradient flows for the Coulomb discrepancy on ℝ𝑑 and 𝕋 𝑑 . It establishes global weak solutions from arbitrary Borel initial probability measures for bounded target densities, together with instantaneous 𝐿 ∞ regularization. On the torus, it proves a global PL inequality for the near-uniform targets and obtains exponential decay of the squared MMD for arbitrary bounded, uniformly positive targets through a defective PL inequality that allows vacuum in the evolving density. In the Euclidean setting, it establishes radial PL coercivity and exponential convergence under source-support inclusion and target-positivity assumptions, while dynamical persistence and static constructions rule out any initial-data-uniform multiplicative decay modulus or global PL inequality on the unrestricted whole-space class. It also proves rigidity of Lagrangian critical points whenever (𝜌 − 𝜇) + is absolutely continuous and, in dimension two, qualitative convergence to the target in narrow, weak-∗ 𝐿 ∞, and negative-Sobolev topologies. These Coulomb-specific results overlap with the present work only for 𝑑 = 𝑞 = 1 , and their Cauchy theory supplies that endpoint case in Theorem 1.2. We also take this opportunity to advertise two closely related works in preparation. Together with the preceding Coulomb paper, these projects address overlapping but complementary aspects of the questions considered here. The two works in preparation are not used in the proofs of the main results of the present paper. The first, by Chodron de Courcel and the first author, studies the unconfined, target-free qua-dratic Wasserstein gradient flow of sub-Coulomb Riesz interaction energies in the range −2 < 𝑠 < 𝑑 − 2 [CR26a]. This work constructs canonical global weak solutions in the scaling-critical and sub-critical Lebesgue classes, proves uniqueness in the corresponding Eulerian class and monotonicity of lower Lebesgue norms, and shows that higher Lebesgue integrability is not created. This absence of any hypercontractivity is in stark contrast to the Coulomb/super-Coulomb case. It also develops a radial measure-valued well-posedness theory on annuli. The aforementioned results also apply to the confined setting, including the MMD target case, under suitable assumptions on the confine-ment. The main contrast with the companion Coulomb case is that the sub-Coulomb evolution is transport-dominated and preserves, rather than improves, Lebesgue singularities. The second, joint work of the first author with Hess-Childs and Serfaty, studies the optimal em-pirical quantization problem for homogeneous and screened Riesz MMDs and diagonal-excluded modulated energies [HRS26]. Given a target probability measure 𝜇 and a sample size 𝑁 , the empir-ical quantization problem asks for points (𝑥 𝑖 )𝑖∈[𝑁] minimizing the prescribed discrepancy or energy between 𝜇 and the equal-weight empirical measure 1𝑁 

∑𝑁 𝑖=1 𝛿 𝑥 𝑖 ; see [GL00] for the classical theory. The work [HRS26] proves fixed-cardinality existence throughout the homogeneous Riesz and loga-rithmic range, upper and lower bounds for the quantization rate which match in their dependence on the number of samples, and separation estimates for empirical minimizers. It also treats a larger class of (screened) Riesz-type kernels. This work is a static/equilibrium complement to the present dynamical analysis. We mention that, for energy kernels, overlapping results were recently obtained independently by Colasanto, Focardi, Fornasier, and Mattesini [Col+26]. 1.4. Organization of the paper. Section 2 gives the Fourier–Sobolev representation used through-out the paper. Section 3 proves the unified continuum well-posedness theorem and the energy– dissipation identity. Section 4 establishes particle noncollision, fixed-𝑁 asymptotic criticality, particle-to-continuum criticality with discrete nonconcentration required only when 0 < 𝑞 < 1 ,collision-free saddle equilibria, and the mean-field estimate. Section 5 proves natural-moment rigidity across the energy-kernel family, treats compact-positive-part and radial rigidity in the residual regime 𝑑 = 3 , 0 < 𝑞 < 1 , gives one-dimensional counterexamples, and then proves asymptotic criticality and conditional target convergence. Sections 6 and 7 give obstructions to global convergence, while Section 8 gives explicit one-dimensional particle dynamics. Further questions appear in Section 9; Appendix A contains the auxiliary MMD results cited above. WASSERSTEIN GRADIENT FLOWS OF MAXIMUM MEAN DISCREPANCY WITH ENERGY KERNELS 11

Well-posedness and properties of solutions We prove Theorem 1.2. We first reduce the endpoint 𝑑 = 𝑞 = 1 to the Coulomb Cauchy theory of [CR26b]. In the remaining range 𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2 > 0 , finite-𝑝 solutions are constructed by adapting the aggregation-equation strategy of [BLR11, Lau07]; the case 𝑝 = ∞ is then obtained by applying the finite-𝑝 theory at an auxiliary exponent and propagating an 𝐿 ∞ bound along characteristics.

Since 𝑇 is arbitrary, the finite-𝑝 solution is global and belongs to the class of Definition 3.1. 3.4. Uniqueness. Uniqueness is a consequence of the following Dobrushin-type stability estimate. The argument only uses the solution class already obtained above: the associated velocities are bounded and globally Lipschitz in space on each finite time interval, and the solutions are repre-sented by their characteristic flows. The stability argument requires pointwise control of kernel-gradient differences, which we record first.

Before recording the higher-regularity obstruction below, we note that the same computation as in Section 4 gives an MMD stability estimate for two continuum solutions with the same target. For different targets, an additional forcing term involving the target mismatch appears; we do not formulate that refinement here. 

Remark 3.9 (Coulomb higher-regularity obstruction) . The admissible integrability range in Theo-rem 1.2 should not be interpreted as giving uniform-in-time control of stronger spatial norms. At the Coulomb endpoint, smooth compactly supported radial source and target densities can gen-erate solutions whose Hölder seminorms grow exponentially. The same construction yields un-bounded growth of every supercritical Sobolev norm. This Coulomb-specific phenomenon, includ-ing the one-dimensional mechanism previously recorded here, is proved in [CR26b, Theorem 1.2 and Proposition 2.5]. 22 MATTHEW ROSENZWEIG, DEJAN SLEPČEV AND LIHAN WANG 

4. Particle dynamics and mean-field estimate We prove Theorems 1.5 and 1.8 and Proposition 1.6 in this section. The noncollision and asymptotic-separation arguments exploit the repulsive sign through a centered second-moment estimate for particles approaching a common collision point. The saddle constructions combine explicit collinear equilibria with symmetry-constrained polygonal configurations. The passage from discrete to continuum criticality in Proposition 4.3 rests on pair symmetrization and regime-dependent control of the resulting two-point integral: bounded continuity at 𝑞 = 1, moment control for 1 < 𝑞 < 2 , and a discrete Riesz bound for 0 < 𝑞 < 1 . Energy dissipation and the MMD topology yield the uniform-in-time consistency result in Corollary 4.9. The proof of Corollary 4.7 obtains limits along sequences 𝑁 𝑘 → ∞ and 𝑡 𝑘 → ∞ by selecting exact critical configurations from the fixed-𝑁 𝜔 -limit sets, establishing tightness from uniform energy and moment bounds, and then applying the closure result; Lemma 4.5 supplies the required uniform energy bound for compactly supported targets. When 0 < 𝑞 < 1 , Lemma 4.4 and Proposition 4.6 give two sufficient criteria for the required Riesz bound. The mean-field estimate follows the modulated-energy approach reviewed in [Ser26, Ros25]: the square of the MMD distance between 𝜌 𝑁 𝑡 and 𝜌 𝑡 is a coercive quantity whose growth is controlled by a transport commutator and Grönwall’s inequality. For a configuration 𝑋 𝑁 = (𝑥 𝑖 )𝑖∈[𝑁] , set 𝜌 𝑁 ∶= 1𝑁 

∑𝑁 𝑖=1 𝛿 𝑥 𝑖 , the time-independent analogue of the empirical measure 𝜌 𝑁 𝑡 in (1.23). When 𝜌 𝑁 and 𝜌 have finite 𝑞 -moment, the representation (1.7) gives (4.1) MMD 2𝑞 (𝜌 𝑁 , 𝜌) = 12

ˆ 

> (ℝ 𝑑 )2

𝐾(𝑥 − 𝑦) d(𝜌 𝑁 − 𝜌)(𝑥) d(𝜌 𝑁 − 𝜌)(𝑦), 𝐾(𝑥) = −|𝑥| 𝑞 .

We call this the modulated energy. Compared with the Coulomb (and more generally singular Riesz) modulated energy, the energy itself does not require an excision of the diagonal: 𝐾 is contin-uous and 𝐾(0) = 0 , so the empirical self-interaction terms in (4.1) vanish. This observation does not 

remove the diagonal issue from the particle ODE or from the differentiated identity, because ∇𝐾(0) 

may be undefined or infinite. The velocity convention below is therefore diagonal-free. Accordingly, we use the following solution concept.

Since 𝑏 = −𝑁∇ℰ 𝑁 on 𝒟 𝑁 , every subsequential limit is a collision-free critical configuration. Finally, for each 𝑇 ≥ 0 , the tail orbit {𝑋 𝑡 𝑁 ∶ 𝑡 ≥ 𝑇} is the continuous image of the interval [𝑇, ∞) 

and is therefore path-connected; hence its closure is connected. These closures, (4.45) {𝑋 𝑡 𝑁 ∶ 𝑡 ≥ 𝑇}, 𝑇 ≥ 0, 

are nonempty, compact, and nested. By (1.19), their intersection is 𝜔(𝑋 𝑁 ). The nested-intersection theorem for compact connected sets shows that 𝜔(𝑋 𝑁 ) is nonempty, compact, and connected. Every one of its elements is critical by the preceding argument, and continuity of ℰ𝑁 places it on the limiting energy level ℰ∞𝑁 . This proves (1.20). The distance conclusion (1.21) follows by contradiction from compactness and (1.20). 

□

If the initial energies are uniformly bounded, energy dissipation gives (4.81); if 𝜇 is compactly supported, the same bound follows from Lemma 4.5. Thus, for compactly supported targets, no additional hypothesis is needed when 1 ≤ 𝑞 < 2 , whereas for 0 < 𝑞 < 1 only the discrete Riesz bound (4.82) remains. □

Remark 4.8 (Scope of the particle criticality results) . For 1 ≤ 𝑞 < 2 , Proposition 4.3 and Corollary 4.7 require no discrete nonconcentration hypothesis. Their conclusion is nevertheless only Lagrangian criticality, not identification of ̄ 𝜌 with 𝜇 : the limit may be singular, whereas the rigidity results below generally assume absolute continuity. The collision-free saddle configurations in Proposition 1.6 likewise preclude an unconditional conclusion that every deterministic particle trajectory converges to a global empirical minimizer. The 0 < 𝑞 < 1 cases of Proposition 4.3, Lemma 4.5, and Corollary 4.7, together with Lemma 4.4 and Proposition 4.6, include 𝑑 = 1 under the hypothesis 𝑝 > 1∕𝑞 . This is consistent with the nested-interval examples in Proposition 5.22: their conclusion is itself Lagrangian criticality, and their target densities belong only to 𝐿 𝑝 for 𝑝 < 2∕(1 + 𝑞) < 1∕𝑞 . Those examples show that unre-stricted one-dimensional rigidity fails, but they do not settle target identification under the stronger regular-target hypothesis used here. The restriction 𝑑 ≥ 2 in Proposition 4.6 is instead intrinsic to that particular result. In dimension one, 𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2 = 𝑞 − 1 < 0 , so the sign in (4.77) reverses; moreover, the target potential with kernel 

|𝑥 − 𝑦| 𝑞−2 is not controlled under 𝑝 > 1∕𝑞 . Thus a lower bound on the Laplacian does not provide the needed discrete Riesz estimate in that regime.

Remark 4.10 (Convergence to one critical configuration) . Theorem 1.5 does not by itself imply that 

𝑋 𝑡 𝑁 converges as 𝑡 → ∞ : compactness gives subsequential limits, but these limits need not a priori coincide. If (4.92) Crit 𝒟 𝑁 ℰ𝑁 ∩ {ℰ 𝑁 = ℰ ∞𝑁 } ∩ {𝑋 𝑡 𝑁 ∶ 𝑡 ≥ 0} 

is totally disconnected (in particular, if it is finite), then the connectedness of 𝜔(𝑋 𝑁 ) forces it to be a singleton, and 𝑋 𝑡 𝑁 converges to one collision-free critical configuration. The same conclusion follows if ℰ𝑁 satisfies a Kurdyka–Łojasiewicz inequality in a neighborhood of 𝜔(𝑋 𝑁 ): such an in-equality yields finite trajectory length. 3

With global collision-free particle dynamics and its fixed-𝑁 asymptotics established, we next ex-hibit collision-free saddle equilibria before turning to the mean-field estimate.

ℰ𝑁 is nonempty and compact. Since each saddle constructed above has a negative Hessian direction, it is not a global minimizer and therefore has positive distance from this compact set. Its constant particle trajectory consequently does not approach the set of global minimizers. □

Remark 4.11 (The one-dimensional energy-distance case) . When 𝑑 = 𝑞 = 1 , the target term in (4.94) is convex and the pair-interaction term is affine on every ordered chamber. Hence ℰ𝑁 is convex on each ordered chamber, so every collision-free critical configuration minimizes on its chamber. By permutation symmetry and continuity on the chamber closures, it is a global minimizer. Thus the restriction 𝑑 ≥ 2 in Proposition 1.6 is essential. We next prove the commutator estimate which controls the last term in (4.142). At a schematic level, the Coulomb/super-Coulomb regime 𝑑 + 𝑞 ≤ 2 is handled directly by the unlocalized commutator estimate of the first author and Serfaty. In the remaining regime, repeated integration by parts transfers derivatives from the zero-mean inputs to the kernel until the terminal differentiated kernel has Riesz exponent 𝑠 𝑚 ∈ [𝑑 − 2, 𝑑) . The estimate of Nguyen, the first author, and Serfaty controls this terminal term, while the lower-order terms are handled by the homoge-neous Kato–Ponce and Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequalities. The only endpoint obstruction in the iterative physical-space integration-by-parts proof occurs when 𝑞 = 1 and 𝑑 is odd. We avoid the gap by choosing the number of integrations by parts so that the terminal kernel is in the Coulomb/super-Coulomb range and then invoking the Calderón commutator argument of Nguyen, the first author, and Serfaty [NRS22, Proposition 3.1] for the terminal term. 38 MATTHEW ROSENZWEIG, DEJAN SLEPČEV AND LIHAN WANG

5. Stationary solutions and critical points Critical points arise naturally from the question of stationary states for the Wasserstein gradient flow (1.2). A stationary solution is, in the weakest sense, a time-independent distributional solu-tion of the continuity equation. To relate this distributional notion of stationarity to variational criticality, we use intrinsic first-variation notions close to the terminology of Boufadène and Vialard [BV25, Definitions 3.1–3.2]. Two distinctions are important. First, a Lagrangian condition says that the velocity field vanishes on the mass actually carried by the measure. Second, a Wasserstein crit-icality condition says that all sufficiently small proximal/JKO steps are blocked. The second notion is stronger in general and is not needed for the constructions below. The stationary picture is rigid across almost the entire energy-kernel family. We first treat the energy-distance kernel in odd dimensions using locality of the associated polyharmonic operator, and then obtain the near-complete natural-moment classification by a Riesz-potential argument. We treat the remaining regime 𝑑 = 3 , 0 < 𝑞 < 1 separately and contrast its conditional rigidity with explicit one-dimensional non-minimizing critical points. Finally, we combine the energy– dissipation identity with compactness to obtain asymptotic criticality without additional long-time bounds for 1 ≤ 𝑞 < 2 , and under uniform bounds for 0 < 𝑞 < 1 ; the corresponding rigidity results then yield conditional convergence to the target. 

Definition 5.1 (Lagrangian critical point) . Let 𝜌, 𝜇 ∈ 𝒫(ℝ 𝑑 ), and let 𝐸 𝜇 be the MMD energy with kernel 𝐾 . We interpret the first variation 𝐾 ∗ (𝜌 − 𝜇) modulo additive constants. In particular, for

This integral is locally absolutely convergent for 0 < 𝑞 ≤ 1 and, for 1 < 𝑞 < 2 , whenever 𝜌, 𝜇 ∈ 𝒫 𝑞−1 (ℝ 𝑑 ); these conditions hold in every application below. If the ordinary convolution is defined, then

5.1. Odd-dimensional local rigidity for energy distance. We first specialize to the energy-distance kernel 𝐾(𝑧) = −|𝑧| . Boufadène–Vialard prove an interior rigidity statement for this kernel in odd dimensions: a Lagrangian critical point agrees with the target on the interior of its support; see [BV25, Theorem 3.4]. If the critical point is absolutely continuous, the same local mechanism gives a global statement without any regularity assumption on the target. The odd-dimensional restriction belongs to this local proof: for odd 𝑑 , the function |𝑥| is, up to normalization, a fundamental solution for the polyharmonic operator (−∆) 𝑚 with 𝑚 = (𝑑 + 1)∕2 , i.e., (5.6) (−∆) 𝑚 (𝐶 𝑑 |𝑥| ) = 𝛿 0 in 𝒟 ′(ℝ 𝑑 )

for some 𝐶 𝑑 ≠ 0 .

{𝑤 ∗ = 0} , and hence ℒ𝑑 -a.e. on 𝐴 . It follows that 𝑟 = 𝑔 ℒ 𝑑 -a.e. on 𝐴 .

5.2. Natural-moment rigidity for energy kernels. The preceding odd-dimensional energy-distance rigidity result is the local polyharmonic case of a broader Riesz-potential mechanism. The following result establishes natural-moment rigidity except when 𝑑 ∈ {1, 3} and 0 < 𝑞 < 1 . The residual three-dimensional regime is treated separately in Theorem 5.17. 

Theorem 5.4 (Rigidity under the 𝑞 -moment condition) . Let 𝑑 ≥ 1 and 0 < 𝑞 < 2 . Assume 𝜇, 𝜌 ∈ 𝒫 𝑞 (ℝ 𝑑 ), 𝜌 ≪ ℒ 𝑑 , and that 𝜌 is a Lagrangian critical point of 𝐸 𝜇 . Then 𝜌 = 𝜇 in each of the following regimes: 

(i) 𝑑 is even; 

(ii) 𝑑 is odd and 1 ≤ 𝑞 < 2 ;

(iii) 𝑑 ≥ 5 is odd and 0 < 𝑞 < 1 .

Write ℳ(ℝ 𝑑 ) for the space of finite signed Borel measures on ℝ𝑑 and ℳ+(ℝ 𝑑 ) for its positive cone. Throughout the Riesz-potential argument, set 𝜈 ∶= 𝜌 − 𝜇 and 𝑢 ∶= 𝐾 ∗ 𝜈 . The local Sobolev regularity of 𝑢 follows from the moment assumptions alone; it does not use absolute continuity of either measure. The exact source-dependent condition used below is (5.17) 𝜈 + ≪ ℒ 𝑑 , ∇𝑢 = 0 𝜈 +-a.e. The assumptions of Theorem 5.4 imply this condition because 𝜈 + = (𝜌−𝜇) + ≤ 𝜌 . Thus the moment assumptions provide the regularity of 𝑢 , while absolute continuity of 𝜌 and Lagrangian criticality provide precisely the two conditions in (5.17). When 𝑠 ∉ ℕ , set (5.18) 𝑠 ∶= 𝑑 + 𝑞 2 , 𝑚 ∶= ⌊𝑠⌋, 𝛼 ∶= 2(𝑠 − 𝑚) = 𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2𝑚 ∈ (0, 2). 

The equality 𝑠 ∈ ℕ occurs exactly when 𝑑 is odd and 𝑞 = 1 ; that case has already been proved in Proposition 5.3. We now treat the rigidity regimes by a common Riesz-potential reduction. We first derive from Lagrangian criticality an equality of Riesz potentials on the positive part of the discrepancy. For 0 < 𝛼 < 𝑑 and 𝜆 ∈ ℳ(ℝ 𝑑 ), denote the Riesz potential of order 𝛼 of 𝜆 by (5.19) ℐ𝛼 𝜆(𝑥) ∶= 𝑐 𝑑,𝛼

We next show that the resulting Riesz contact is rigid. The key potential-theoretic ingredient is the complete maximum principle for Riesz kernels. If 𝑛 ≥ 2 , 0 < 𝛽 ≤ 2 , 𝛽 < 𝑛 , 𝜆 is a positive measure of finite 𝛽 -Riesz energy, and 𝛾 ∈ ℳ +(ℝ 𝑛 ), then (5.35) ℐ𝛽 𝜆 ≤ ℐ 𝛽 𝛾 𝜆 -a.e. ⟹ ℐ𝛽 𝜆 ≤ ℐ 𝛽 𝛾 everywhere .

This is the zero-additive-constant case of the complete maximum principle in [Zor23, Theorem 2.2]. The one-dimensional case needed below is obtained by embedding ℝ as a line in ℝ2: an order-𝛼 

kernel on ℝ, 0 < 𝛼 < 1 , is the restriction of the order-(𝛼 + 1) kernel on ℝ2.A finite Riesz contact need not itself have finite energy, so we first approximate its positive mea-sure from below by finite-energy measures to which the complete maximum principle applies.

see [Ste70, Chapter V, §1]. Since ℐ𝛼 𝜂 = 0 as a locally integrable distribution, (5.50) yields 𝜂 = 0 . □

Combining the finite-energy exhaustion and the complete maximum principle with the preced-ing one-sign cancellation yields the following contact rigidity statement. No absolute-continuity hypothesis is required. 48 MATTHEW ROSENZWEIG, DEJAN SLEPČEV AND LIHAN WANG 

Proposition 5.11 (Riesz contact rigidity) . Let 𝑑 ≥ 1 and 0 < 𝛼 < min{2, 𝑑} . Let 𝜎, 𝜏 ∈ ℳ +(ℝ 𝑑 )

have equal mass and satisfy 

(5.51) 

ˆ

> ℝ𝑑

|𝑥| 𝛼 d(𝜎 + 𝜏)(𝑥) < ∞. 

If the canonical Riesz potentials 6 satisfy 

(5.52) ℐ𝛼 𝜎 = ℐ 𝛼 𝜏 < ∞ 𝜎 -a.e. ,

then 𝜎 = 𝜏 .Proof. Let 𝜎 𝑗 ↑ 𝜎 be the exhaustion from Lemma 5.9. Since 𝜎 𝑗 ≤ 𝜎 , the contact equality implies (5.53) ℐ𝛼 𝜎 𝑗 ≤ ℐ 𝛼 𝜎 = ℐ 𝛼 𝜏 𝜎 𝑗 -a.e. For 𝑑 ≥ 2 , the complete maximum principle [Zor23, Theorem 2.2] yields (5.54) ℐ𝛼 𝜎 𝑗 ≤ ℐ 𝛼 𝜏 everywhere on ℝ𝑑 .

For 𝑑 = 1 , push the measures forward by 𝑥 ↦ (𝑥, 0) into ℝ2 and apply the same theorem at order 

𝛼 + 1 ∈ (1, 2) ; the resulting kernel restricted to the line is a positive multiple of the order-𝛼 kernel on ℝ. Monotone convergence gives (5.55) ℐ𝛼 𝜎 ≤ ℐ 𝛼 𝜏 everywhere, in the extended-valued sense .

The two potentials are locally integrable and hence finite ℒ𝑑 -a.e. Therefore ℐ𝛼 (𝜎 − 𝜏) ≤ 0 ℒ 𝑑 -a.e. The signed measure 𝜎 − 𝜏 has zero mass and finite 𝛼 -moment, so Proposition 5.10 gives 𝜎 = 𝜏 . □

The preceding results immediately yield the single-contact mechanism. 

Theorem 5.12 (Single-contact rigidity) . Let 𝑑 ≥ 1 , let 0 < 𝑞 < 2 , let 𝑚 ≥ 1 be an integer, and set 

(5.56) 𝛼 = 𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2𝑚 ∈ (0, min{2, 𝑑}).

(5.57) 𝜈 ∶= 𝜌 − 𝜇, 𝑢 ∶= 𝐾 ∗ 𝜈.

If ∇𝑢 = 0 𝜈 +-a.e., then 𝜌 = 𝜇 .Proof. By Proposition 5.8, the Jordan parts 𝜈 + and 𝜈 − have finite contact ℐ𝛼 𝜈 + = ℐ 𝛼 𝜈 − 𝜈 +-a.e. They have equal mass by Lemma 5.7, and the moment assumption gives the finite 𝛼 -moment required by Proposition 5.11. Hence 𝜈 + = 𝜈 −, and therefore 𝜌 = 𝜇 . □

Corollary 5.13 (Regimes for single-contact rigidity) . In the setting of Theorem 5.4, single-contact rigidity applies in each of the following cases: 

(i) if 𝑑 = 2𝑘 is even, take 𝑚 = 𝑘 and 𝛼 = 𝑞 ;

(ii) if 𝑑 = 2𝑘 + 1 is odd and 1 < 𝑞 < 2 , take 𝑚 = 𝑘 + 1 and 𝛼 = 𝑞 − 1 ;

(iii) if 𝑑 = 2𝑘 + 1 ≥ 3 is odd and 0 < 𝑞 < 1 , take 𝑚 = 𝑘 and 𝛼 = 𝑞 + 1 , provided in this last case that 𝜌, 𝜇 ∈ 𝒫 𝑞+1 (ℝ 𝑑 ).Proof. By Lemma 5.7, the assumptions of Theorem 5.4 imply (5.17). The displayed values of 𝛼 follow from 𝛼 = 𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2𝑚 . In case (i), the required residual moment is exactly the natural 𝑞 -moment. In case (ii), a finite 𝑞 -moment implies a finite (𝑞 − 1) -moment. Case (iii) is the additional-moment statement. □

Only case (iii) fails to close at the natural moment. In odd dimensions at least five, the Lagrangian condition supplies a second contact that removes this loss.

The first finite contact, the exhaustion from Lemma 5.9, and the complete maximum principle give, exactly as in the proof of Proposition 5.11, (5.64) ℐ𝛼 𝜈 + ≤ ℐ 𝛼 𝜈 − everywhere .

Define (5.65) 𝑉 ∶= −ℐ 𝛼 𝜈 ≥ 0 ℒ𝑑 -a.e. Assume for the sake of a contradiction that 𝜈 ≠ 0 . Riesz inversion implies that 𝑉 does not vanish 

ℒ𝑑 -a.e. Thus 𝑉 > 0 on a set of positive Lebesgue measure. Moreover, 𝜈 + ≠ 0 : otherwise 𝜈 ≤ 0 and

holds for every positive measure 𝜆 by Fubini-Tonelli and the classical convolution identity; see [Ste70, Chapter V, §1]. Applying it separately to 𝜈 ± at the selected point, where both sides are finite, gives (5.67) ℐ2(𝑉 ℒ 𝑑 )(𝑥) = ℐ 𝛼+2 𝜈 −(𝑥) − ℐ 𝛼+2 𝜈 +(𝑥) = 0.

Proof of Theorem 5.4. By Lemma 5.7, the absolute continuity of 𝜌 and the Lagrangian condition imply (5.17). Let 𝑑 = 2𝑘 be even. Remark 5.15 (Role of absolute continuity) . The moment assumptions alone give all local Sobolev regularity used in the integer reduction. Absolute continuity of 𝜌 enters the theorem only through the implications (5.68) (𝜌 − 𝜇) + ≪ ℒ 𝑑 , ∇𝑢 = 0 (𝜌 − 𝜇) +-a.e. ,

which follow from (𝜌 − 𝜇) + ≤ 𝜌 . Indeed, writing 𝜈 + = ℎℒ 𝑑 and 𝐵 ∶= {ℎ > 0} , the Lagrangian condition implies ∇𝑢 = 0 ℒ 𝑑 -a.e. on 𝐵 . Sobolev locality can therefore be applied on 𝐵 , and the resulting finite Riesz contact holds 𝜈 +-a.e. because 𝜈 + is concentrated on 𝐵 . From that point on, Proposition 5.11 uses no absolute continuity. 50 MATTHEW ROSENZWEIG, DEJAN SLEPČEV AND LIHAN WANG 

No hypothesis of compact support, positivity on the support, bounded density, or absolute conti-nuity of the target enters the proof. The relevant positive-density set is merely measurable, and the singular part of 𝜇 is permitted. 5.3. The residual three-dimensional regime. Among the regimes with 𝑑+𝑞 > 2 and (𝑑+𝑞)∕2 ∉ ℕ, the only regime not covered by Theorem 5.4 is 𝑑 = 3 and 0 < 𝑞 < 1 . Finite moments of order 

𝑞 + 1 close the single-contact argument directly. At the natural 𝑞 -th moment level, it suffices that 

supp(𝜌 − 𝜇) + be compact. If 𝜌 − 𝜇 is invariant under rotations about some point, the radial flux identity together with the Lagrangian criticality condition forces (𝜌−𝜇) + to be compactly supported, reducing the radial case to the compact-support case. We first record the equilibrium-potential facts used by the compactness argument and then consolidate the three alternatives.

Remark 5.18 (Necessary features of a counterexample) . Any nontrivial counterexample to natural-moment rigidity in the residual three-dimensional regime must have nonradial discrepancy and unbounded support of its positive part (𝜌 − 𝜇) +.

Corollary 5.19 (Compact-support rigidity at noninteger order) . Let 𝑑 ≥ 1 and 0 < 𝑞 < 2 satisfy 

(5.86) 𝑑 + 𝑞 > 2, 𝑑 + 𝑞 2 ∉ ℕ. 

Assume 𝜇, 𝜌 ∈ 𝒫 𝑞 (ℝ 𝑑 ), 𝜌 ≪ ℒ 𝑑 , and that 𝜌 is a Lagrangian critical point of 𝐸 𝜇 . If supp 𝜌 is compact, then 𝜌 = 𝜇 .Proof. All parameter regimes covered by the corollary fall under Theorem 5.4, except 𝑑 = 3 and 

0 < 𝑞 < 1 . In the latter case, the domination (𝜌 − 𝜇) + ≤ 𝜌 from Lemma 5.7 gives (5.87) supp ((𝜌 − 𝜇) +) ⊆ supp 𝜌. 

The compact-positive-part alternative in Theorem 5.17 therefore gives 𝜌 = 𝜇 . □

Remark 5.20 . In particular, in the range of Corollary 5.19, if the target 𝜇 has unbounded support, then it admits no compactly supported absolutely continuous Lagrangian critical point. 5.4. Flexibility below the energy-distance endpoint. The preceding results leave two regimes with 0 < 𝑞 < 1 outside unconditional natural-moment rigidity, but only one is known to be flexible. For 𝑑 = 3 and 0 < 𝑞 < 1 , any unresolved natural-moment case must have nonradial discrepancy and unbounded support of its positive part; rigidity holds under finite moments of order 𝑞 +1 , when the positive part is compactly supported, or when the discrepancy is radial. In contrast, for 𝑑 = 1 

and 0 < 𝑞 < 1 , compactly supported non-minimizing critical points exist. For the energy-kernel interaction profile (5.88) 𝐾(𝑧) = −|𝑧| 𝑞 , 0 < 𝑞 < 1, 

there are explicit absolutely continuous Lagrangian critical points which are not minimizers. The construction is a nested-interval version of the classical equilibrium-measure mechanism. We record the normalization and the short variational consequence of the explicit interval equilibrium formula that are used below. 

Lemma 5.21 (Constant-potential density on an interval) . Let 0 < 𝑞 < 1 and 𝑅 > 0 . Define

is constant for 𝑥 ∈ (−𝑅, 𝑅) .Proof. For 𝑅 = 1 , the asserted density is the (−𝑞) -equilibrium measure of [−1, 1] ; see [BHS19, Proposition 4.6.1] and [CL25, Appendix A]. The equilibrium Euler–Lagrange condition and conti-nuity of the potential give its constancy on (−1, 1) . Scaling gives the result for general 𝑅 , while the endpoint behavior yields the stated 𝐿 𝑝 range. □WASSERSTEIN GRADIENT FLOWS OF MAXIMUM MEAN DISCREPANCY WITH ENERGY KERNELS 53

where 𝜂 𝑞,𝑅 is defined by (5.89) . Then 𝜌 𝑟 is a Lagrangian critical point of the MMD energy with target

is constant on (−𝑟, 𝑟) , and its weak derivative is zero there. The boundary points have zero 𝜌 𝑟 -mass, so (5.94) ∇𝐾 ∗ (𝜌 𝑟 − 𝜇) = 0 𝜌 𝑟 -a.e. Thus 𝜌 𝑟 is a Lagrangian critical point. The measures 𝜇 and 𝜌 𝑟 are obviously distinct, compactly supported probability measures. By the Fourier–Sobolev representation in Lemma 2.1, the MMD energy is strictly positive unless the two measures agree. Hence 𝐸 𝜇 (𝜌 𝑟 ) > 𝐸 𝜇 (𝜇) = 0 , so 𝜌 𝑟 is not a minimizer. □

Remark 5.23 . The preceding proposition is deliberately stated only in the one-dimensional energy-kernel MMD range 0 < 𝑞 < 1 . Classical Riesz equilibrium measures yield analogous constant-potential constructions in other super-Coulomb regimes; for background, see [BHS19, Chapter 4]. Those kernels, however, do not coincide with the positive-order energy-kernel MMD family studied in the rest of the paper. The Coulomb endpoint 𝑞 = 1 is also excluded. As 𝑞 ↑ 1 , the measures 

𝜂 𝑞,𝑅 d𝑥 converge weakly to 12 (𝛿 −𝑅 + 𝛿 𝑅 ). Thus the nested-interval construction leaves the absolutely continuous class at the endpoint and does not contradict the rigidity result above. 5.5. Asymptotic criticality and conditional target convergence. The following is a LaSalle-type consequence of the energy–dissipation identity. For related abstract results, see [CGW23]. For 

1 ≤ 𝑞 < 2 , the energy itself supplies the moment bound needed for asymptotic compactness. When 

0 < 𝑞 < 1 , the uniform moment and 𝐿 𝑝 hypotheses are used below to pass to the singular critical-point equation.

Corollary 5.25 (Conditional convergence for energy kernels) . Let 𝑑 ≥ 1 and let 0 < 𝑞 < 2 satisfy 

𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2 ≥ 0 . Let 

(5.119) 𝑟 ≥ max{1, 𝑞}, 𝑝 𝑐 ≤ 𝑝 ≤ ∞, 𝑝 > 𝑝 𝑐 if 𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2 > 0. 

If 𝑑 = 3 and 0 < 𝑞 < 1 , assume in addition either that 𝑟 ≥ 𝑞+1 or that 𝜌 0 and 𝜇 are invariant under rotations about a common point. Suppose that 𝜇, 𝜌 0 ∈ 𝒫 𝑟 (ℝ 𝑑 ) ∩ 𝐿 𝑝 (ℝ 𝑑 ) are probability densities, and let 𝜌 𝑡 be the solution of (1.2) . If (5.95) holds, then 

(5.120) 𝜌 𝑡 ⇀ 𝜇 narrowly , MMD 𝑞 (𝜌 𝑡 , 𝜇) ⟶ 0. 

Proof. Every element ̄ 𝜌 ∈ 𝜔(𝜌 0) is an 𝐿 𝑝 probability density in 𝒫 𝑟 (ℝ 𝑑 ) and a Lagrangian critical point by Theorem 5.24. Suppose first that either 𝑑 ≠ 3 or 𝑞 ≥ 1 . Since 𝑟 ≥ max{1, 𝑞} , both ̄ 𝜌 and 𝜇 

belong to 𝒫 𝑞 (ℝ 𝑑 ). The admissible pairs (𝑑, 𝑞) lie in one of the following regimes of Theorem 5.4: 𝑑 

is even; 𝑑 is odd and 𝑞 ≥ 1 ; or 𝑑 ≥ 5 is odd and 0 < 𝑞 < 1 . Hence ̄ 𝜌 = 𝜇 .Suppose now that 𝑑 = 3 and 0 < 𝑞 < 1 . If 𝑟 ≥ 𝑞 + 1 , then ̄ 𝜌, 𝜇 ∈ 𝒫 𝑞+1 (ℝ 3), so Theorem 5.17(i) gives ̄ 𝜌 = 𝜇 . Otherwise, 𝜌 0 and 𝜇 are invariant under rotations about a common point. For any such rotation 𝑇 , rotation invariance of 𝐾 implies that 𝑇 #𝜌 𝑡 solves (1.2) with target 𝑇 #𝜇 = 𝜇 and initial datum 𝑇 #𝜌 0 = 𝜌 0. Uniqueness in Theorem 1.2 therefore gives 𝑇 #𝜌 𝑡 = 𝜌 𝑡 for every 𝑡 ≥ 0 .Pushforward by 𝑇 is continuous for narrow convergence, so every ̄ 𝜌 ∈ 𝜔(𝜌 0) is invariant under the same rotations. Consequently, ̄ 𝜌 − 𝜇 is radial about the common center, and Theorem 5.17(iii) gives ̄ 𝜌 = 𝜇 . Thus 𝜔(𝜌 0) = {𝜇} . Since the orbit is narrowly relatively compact, this singleton 𝜔 -limit set forces 𝜌 𝑡 ⇀ 𝜇 narrowly. Indeed, otherwise there would be 𝜀 > 0 and 𝑡 𝑛 → ∞ such that 

𝑑 BL (𝜌 𝑡 𝑛 , 𝜇) ≥ 𝜀 ; relative compactness would give a subsequence converging narrowly to an element of 𝜔(𝜌 0), necessarily 𝜇 , which is a contradiction. Finally, since 𝑞∕2 < 1 ≤ 𝑟 , narrow convergence and the uniform 𝑟 -th moment bound imply 𝖳 𝑞∕2 (𝜌 𝑡 , 𝜇) → 0 , and Proposition A.3 gives convergence in MMD 𝑞 . □

Remark 5.26 . The only one-dimensional range not covered by the corollary is 0 < 𝑞 < 1 , for which 

𝑑 + 𝑞 − 2 < 0 . In that range, Proposition 5.22 exhibits distinct absolutely continuous Lagrangian critical points. The endpoint 𝑑 = 𝑞 = 1 is covered by the corollary. 6. PL inequalities We record several static obstructions to global Polyak–Łojasiewicz inequalities for the MMD en-ergy. The common mechanism is that the energy may be carried either far from the target or in high-frequency directions where the Wasserstein dissipation is too weak. Throughout the section, constants depending only on fixed kernels, the dimension, the target, and the fixed bump functions may change from line to line. Remark 6.3 . For compactly supported bounded targets, the logarithmic endpoint 𝑞 = 0 in di-mension two follows from the same translated-bump argument used in [CR26b, proof of Propo-sition 1.15]: translating a fixed smooth bump to distance 𝑅 gives energy of order log 𝑅 and bounded dissipation. We omit the repetition. 6.3. Periodic sub-Coulomb kernels. Remark 6.5 (Periodic Coulomb endpoint) . The high-frequency construction in Proposition 6.4 does not cover the Coulomb endpoint. In one dimension, periodic integration by parts gives an exact weighted electric-field identity, so every uniformly positive target satisfies a PL inequality, whereas the inequality may fail when the target vanishes at one point. In higher dimensions, targets that are sufficiently small perturbations of the uniform density satisfy a PL inequality; however, even a prescribed positive lower bound does not yield a PL constant that is uniform over the full target class. We refer to [CR26b] for these results and the associated convergence theory. 7. Long-time convergence Long-time convergence asks whether the flow approaches the target rather than a spurious sta-tionary state, and at what rate. The asymptotic-criticality result Theorem 5.24 is unconditional for 

1 ≤ 𝑞 < 2 , while its 0 < 𝑞 < 1 branch and the subsequent target-identification result use uniform moment and 𝐿 𝑝 bounds. Here we prove the complementary negative statement Proposition 1.11: on ℝ𝑑 , no MMD decay modulus can be uniform over the full admissible class of initial data. The argument is the dynamical counterpart of the far-field PL obstruction: a translated source retains discrepancy of order 𝑅 𝑞 , while a waiting-time estimate keeps it in a far-field neighborhood of its initial location for a time that diverges with 𝑅 .For the proof, fix a nonnegative probability density 𝜃 ∈ 𝐶 ∞𝑐 (𝐵 1(0)) . Then 𝜌 0,𝑅 is a smooth compactly supported probability density, its 𝐿 𝑝 norm is independent of 𝑅 ,and it has finite moments of every order. Proof of Proposition 1.11. With the notation fixed above, suppose, toward a contradiction, that a function 𝛾 satisfying (1.27) exists. Increase 𝑅 0 so that Lemmas 6.1 and 7.1 apply for every 𝑅 ≥ 𝑅 0.For such 𝑅 , Lemma 7.1 and Lemma 6.1 imply (7.10) 𝑐𝑅 𝑞 ≤ MMD 2𝑞 (𝜌 𝑇 𝑅 ,𝑅 , 𝜇) ≤ 𝛾(𝑇 𝑅 )MMD 2𝑞 (𝜌 0,𝑅 , 𝜇) ≤ 𝐶𝑅 𝑞 𝛾(𝑇 𝑅 ). 

Thus 𝛾(𝑇 𝑅 ) ≥ 𝑐∕𝐶 for all sufficiently large 𝑅 . Since 𝑇 𝑅 → ∞ , this contradicts lim 𝑡→∞ 𝛾(𝑡) = 0 . □

Remark 7.2 . Proposition 1.11 does not rule out qualitative convergence, nor does it rule out local convergence estimates whose constants or waiting times depend on the initial datum. It only ex-cludes a global multiplicative rate in MMD with no dependence on the initial support radius or moment size. For compactly supported targets in the overlapping case 𝑑 = 𝑞 = 1 , a compatible Coulomb persistence result is proved in [CR26b, Theorem 1.12 and Corollary 1.13]. 

Remark 7.3 (Radial Coulomb PL inequality) . Coulomb-specific radial reductions lead to substan-tially stronger conclusions than are presently available for the general energy kernels considered here. If the source and target are radial bounded densities, the target has connected support and is bounded above and below there, and the source support is contained in the target support, Chodron de Courcel and the first author prove a PL inequality and exponential convergence [CR26b, The-orem 1.8 and Corollary 1.11]. The structural assumptions are shown to be sharp within the radial class; see [CR26b, Remarks 1.9–1.10]. 8. Two-time-scale phenomenon: a one-dimensional example The obstruction in Proposition 1.11 concerns uniformity over the initial support scale and does not rule out rapid relaxation after a datum-dependent waiting time. Remark 8.1 (Support normalization and noncompact targets) . The choice of [0, 1] is only a normal-ization. If 𝜇 is supported on an interval [𝑎, 𝑏] and its density satisfies 𝜇 ≥ 𝜆 > 0 there, the preceding argument applies with 0 and 1 replaced by 𝑎 and 𝑏 . Compact support is not needed for the cumu-lative distribution function (CDF) reduction (8.4). Subject to the standing moment assumptions, if 𝐹 is continuous and strictly increasing, each particle moves monotonically toward its quantile equilibrium. For a noncompact target, however, a probability density cannot be bounded below uniformly on ℝ. Quantitative estimates therefore combine tail control of the time needed to reach a neighborhood of equilibrium with local lower bounds for the density there; the local behavior of the density near the equilibrium determines the subsequent relaxation rate. 8.2. Since changing a single quantile does not change the underlying measure, obtaining a continuum decay law requires perturbing a positive-measure family of nearby quantiles, which we do not pursue here. 9. Further questions and directions The preceding sections establish global continuum and particle dynamics, a fixed-time mean-field limit, and several obstructions to uniform convergence. The questions below concern what remains beyond this framework: long-time selection and rates, well-posedness at the finite criti-cal exponent, the remaining rigidity and critical-point questions, and uniform-in-time mean-field approximation. For 1 ≤ 𝑞 < 2 , the energy–dissipation identity and Theorem 5.24 already imply that 𝑑 BL (𝜌 𝑡 , 𝒞 𝜇 ) → 0 as 𝑡 → ∞ , but without the uniform bounds the 𝜔 -limit points are not known to be absolutely con-tinuous. A first target-selection question is therefore when the moment and subcritical 𝐿 𝑝 bounds propagate uniformly for all 𝑡 ≥ 0 ; when 0 < 𝑞 < 1 , the same bounds are also used here to pass to the critical-point equation. Whenever the available uniform moment order meets the hypotheses of the rigidity theorem, every 𝜔 -limit point equals the target; in the residual three-dimensional regime, the same conclusion holds at the natural moment level for radial source and target data. Within this uniformly controlled class, the remaining target-identification problem concerns 𝑑 = 3 and 

0 < 𝑞 < 1 with moment order below 𝑞 + 1 and without radial symmetry or a compact-positive-part condition on the limiting critical points. Outside the present well-posedness range, when 𝑑 = 1 and 

0 < 𝑞 < 1 , non-minimizing critical points exist; one may instead ask which of them are dynamically accessible and how their basins of attraction are organized. The failures of a global data-independent rate do not rule out local or data-dependent conver-gence estimates. More precisely, one may seek a flow-invariant class, described by some combina-tion of support, tail, moment, and two-sided density controls, on which a Łojasiewicz-type dissipa-tion inequality (9.1) 𝒟 𝐾 (𝜌 ∣ 𝜇) ≥ 𝑐 ℰ 𝐾 (𝜌 − 𝜇) 1+𝛼 

holds. Here 𝛼 = 0 would yield exponential decay, while 𝛼 > 0 would yield polynomial decay whenever the energy identity is available. Small MMD alone need not preserve the geometric or WASSERSTEIN GRADIENT FLOWS OF MAXIMUM MEAN DISCREPANCY WITH ENERGY KERNELS 65 

density information required for such an estimate, so a central issue is to find natural conditions that propagate along the flow. The compact-geometry results of [Chi+26] provide one model for this local theory. The radial Coulomb theory of [CR26b] further suggests asking how the order and geometry of target degeneracy determine the rate and whether source-support-inclusion estimates survive without radial symmetry. A separate analytic problem occurs at the critical endpoint 𝑝 = 𝑝 𝑐 of the Euclidean well-posedness theory. The critical Lorentz refinement 𝐿 𝑝 𝑐 ,1 should follow from adapting the well-posedness argument of [Chi+26], whose mechanism is not essentially periodic. The sharper question is whether the flow is well posed when the source and target belong only to the critical Lebesgue space 𝐿 𝑝 . At this endpoint the natural velocity control is Osgood rather than Lipschitz, while the target field ∇𝐾 ∗ 𝜇 can have unbounded negative divergence, so the present 𝐿 𝑝 estimate does not close. Does there exist a unique global solution in an appropriate critical solution class, or are stronger assumptions on the target necessary? For the Euclidean energy kernel 𝐾(𝑧) = −|𝑧| 𝑞 , the principal remaining rigidity question is whether the natural 𝑞 -moment assumption suffices when 𝑑 = 3 and 0 < 𝑞 < 1 , or whether this regime admits an absolutely continuous non-minimizing Lagrangian critical point. Any such counterexample must have nonradial discrepancy and unbounded support of its positive part. More generally, one would like to determine under which regularity and support assumptions Lagrangian criticality in the sense of Definition 5.1 agrees with stationarity or with the stronger Wasserstein and JKO notions discussed above. Beyond rigidity, one would like stability criteria and a description of the corresponding basins of attraction. The examples in Proposition 1.6 give exact collision-free particle equilibria with either a singular line-supported limit or the full-dimensional uniform-ball limit. More generally, which continuum Lagrangian critical points arise as limits of exact critical configurations of ℰ𝑁 ? In particular, does every sufficiently regular continuum critical point admit such a recovery sequence, and what stability or nondegeneracy assumptions are necessary? Finally, beyond the well-prepared regime of Corollary 4.9, suppose that MMD 𝑞 (𝜌 𝑁 0 , 𝜌 0) → 0 . The estimate (1.24) propagates the mean-field approximation on intervals [0, 𝑇 𝑁 ] with 𝑇 𝑁 → ∞ cho-sen sufficiently slowly. Particle energy dissipation then selects times 𝑡 𝑁 ∈ [𝑇 𝑁 ∕2, 𝑇 𝑁 ] at which the mean-square particle velocity tends to zero. This growing-window argument applies throughout the present mean-field range; the range 0 < 𝑞 < 1 enters only in the closure step. For 1 ≤ 𝑞 < 2 , Propo-sition 4.3 requires no additional microscopic nonconcentration hypothesis, whereas for 0 < 𝑞 < 1 

it additionally requires a uniform discrete Riesz bound with inverse-power exponent 1 − 𝑞 at those same selected times. Under the corresponding closure hypotheses, this gives particle and contin-uum subsequences with the same Lagrangian-critical limit. Can the required nonconcentration bound when 0 < 𝑞 < 1 be propagated from natural initial data, or can the mean-field comparison be made uniform in time on a stable class? Any such comparison must account for the positive optimal 𝑁 -point quantization error of the target, whose sharp asymptotics are studied in [HRS26], rather than assuming that the particle dy-namics can reach zero discrepancy at fixed 𝑁 . After separating this unavoidable floor, one may ask whether the mean-field and long-time limits commute and whether the empirical-measure images of the particle 𝜔 -limit sets converge to the 𝜔 -limit sets of the continuum flow. Such a result would also require control of discrete metastability and an understanding of which continuum critical points admit recovery sequences of finite-particle equilibria. Appendix A. Proposition A.4 (MMD and transport convergence) . Let 𝑞 ∈ (0, 2) and 𝜇 𝑛 , 𝜇 ∈ 𝒫 𝑞∕2 (ℝ 𝑑 ).

(1) If 𝖳 𝑞∕2 (𝜇 𝑛 , 𝜇) → 0 , then MMD 𝑞 (𝜇 𝑛 , 𝜇) → 0 .

(2) If MMD 𝑞 (𝜇 𝑛 , 𝜇) → 0 , then 𝖳 𝑟 (𝜇 𝑛 , 𝜇) → 0 for every 0 < 𝑟 < 𝑞∕2 .Proof. The first assertion is (A.7). For the second, Proposition A.2 gives convergence and uniform boundedness of every 𝑟 -moment with 0 < 𝑟 < 𝑞∕2 , while Lemma 2.1 gives distributional conver-gence against Schwartz functions. Tightness upgrades this to weak convergence, and weak conver-gence together with convergence of the 𝑟 -moments is equivalent to convergence in 𝖳 𝑟 . □

A.3. Borderline examples. The preceding comparison is sharp at the endpoint 𝑞∕2 , and 𝒫 𝑞∕2 (ℝ 𝑑 )

is not complete for the MMD metric. The following two examples exhibit these failures using the summable sequence (A.17) 𝑎 𝑚 ∶= 𝑓 𝑞 (2 𝑚∕2 ), 𝑓 𝑞 (𝑠) ∶= 𝑠 𝑞 + 𝑠 −𝑞 − (𝑠 − 𝑠 −1 )𝑞 , 𝑚 ≥ 0.

Thus the leading term is 𝑠 −𝑞 for 0 < 𝑞 < 1 , 2𝑠 −1 for 𝑞 = 1 , and 𝑞𝑠 𝑞−2 for 1 < 𝑞 < 2 . In particular, (A.19) 𝑎 𝑚 ≤ 𝐶 𝑞 2−𝑚 min{𝑞,2−𝑞}∕2 ,

so 𝑎 ∈ 𝓁 1(ℕ 0).

Remark A.10 (Limitation of the direct truncation argument) . The proof of Lemma A.9 uses the pointwise positivity of 𝑘 𝑞 (𝑥, 𝑦) = 12 (|𝑥| 𝑞 + |𝑦| 𝑞 − |𝑥 − 𝑦| 𝑞 ), which follows from subadditivity only when 0 < 𝑞 ≤ 1 . For 1 < 𝑞 < 2 ,(A.48) 𝑘 𝑞 (𝑥, −𝑥) = 12 (2 − 2 𝑞 )|𝑥| 𝑞 < 0. 

Thus the monotone truncation argument has no sign content beyond 𝑞 = 1 . The completion state-ment nevertheless remains true in the full range 0 < 𝑞 < 2 ; for 1 < 𝑞 < 2 , the proof below replaces monotonicity by dyadic square-tail control and annular almost orthogonality.

We now combine the preceding truncation and closedness results to prove Proposition A.7. 

Proof of Proposition A.7. The inclusion 𝒫 𝑞∕2 ⊂ ˜ 𝒫 𝑞 follows exactly as in the proof of Lemma 2.1: since 

‖Φ 𝑥 ‖𝐻 𝑞 = 𝐶 1∕2 𝑑,𝑞 |𝑥| 𝑞∕2 , the Bochner integral ´ Φ𝑥 d𝜇(𝑥) is well-defined in 𝐻 𝑞 whenever 𝜇 ∈ 𝒫 𝑞∕2 ,and the resulting 𝐻 𝑞 element has continuous representative ˆ𝜇 − 1 .The density of the compactly supported truncations in ˜𝒫 𝑞 (ℝ 𝑑 ) is Lemma A.9 for 0 < 𝑞 ≤ 1 and Lemma A.13 for 1 < 𝑞 < 2 . The reverse implication in (A.29) is immediate: if the displayed 𝐻 𝑞 

norm tends to zero, then ˆ𝜇 − ˆ 𝜇 𝑅 ∈ 𝐻 𝑞 for all sufficiently large 𝑅 ; since ˆ𝜇 𝑅 − 1 ∈ 𝐻 𝑞 , it follows that 

ˆ𝜇 − 1 ∈ 𝐻 𝑞 . The reverse inclusion in the completion identification follows from Lemma A.14. Since Lemma 2.1 identifies MMD 𝑞 (𝜇, 𝜈) with ‖ˆ 𝜇 − ˆ 𝜈‖ 𝐻 𝑞 , the stated completion identification follows. □
